Yeah… I don’t really enjoy much of anything now. I used to look forward to choir, you know, and there was pickup basketball sometimes—nothing serious, just guys running around like our knees were still 25. But now I just… I can’t make myself care enough to go. People text me about choir or ask if I’m coming by, and I’ll see it light up and just turn the phone over. I don’t even answer half the time. It’s like the part of me that used to want those things just shut off.

Yeah… slowed down, definitely. Everything feels like I’m moving through mud, even little things like getting up from a chair or walking to the kitchen.

Not even because of one clear thing. It just comes over me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About four months, I think. It kind of started after my wife went back to working full-time, and I was home most weekdays by myself. Funny, I used to think I’d like the quiet—no TV on, nobody asking where the keys are—but it got… too quiet, I guess. So yeah, around four months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t really enjoy much of anything now. I used to look forward to choir, you know, and there was pickup basketball sometimes—nothing serious, just guys running around like our knees were still 25. And I liked working on cars, even just messing with an engine for an afternoon. Grilling for the family too… I used to make a whole thing of it, checking the charcoal, telling everybody not to touch the lid.\n\nBut now I just… I can’t make myself care enough to go. People text me about choir or ask if I’m coming by, and I’ll see it light up and just turn the phone over. I don’t even answer half the time. It’s like the part of me that used to want those things just shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean… I don’t like saying it out loud. The thought keeps coming that I should just be dead. Like everybody would be better off if I wasn’t here taking up space.\n\nAnd when I’m alone, that’s when it scares me. Because it’s not just a passing thing sometimes—I start thinking I might actually do something to make it happen. Then I’ll notice some stupid thing, like a crack in the wall or the sound the fridge is making, and I try to focus on that instead, but… yeah. The thoughts are there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how quickly or slowly you're moving compared to before you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… slowed down, definitely. Everything feels like I’m moving through mud, even little things like getting up from a chair or walking to the kitchen.\n\nAnd when someone asks me something, I hear them, I do… but it’s like the words have to travel a long way before I can answer. My wife will be standing there waiting, and I’m just… stuck. It reminds me of when my old truck wouldn’t turn over right away, you know, that pause before it catches—except it’s me. I don’t mean to ignore people. It just takes me a long time to get anything out.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really been keeping track of the number. The scale’s in the bathroom, but it’s one of those old ones that never sits right on the tile, so I don’t trust it much anyway.\n\nMy pants maybe feel a little different some days, but then laundry does that too, you know? My wife used to tell me I should write things down, like mileage on the car or what we spent at the hardware store, but I never did. So… no, I can’t really say I’ve lost a significant amount. So yeah, around four months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t really enjoy much of anything now. I used to look forward to choir, you know, and there was pickup basketball sometimes—nothing serious, just guys running around like our knees were still 25. And I liked working on cars, even just messing with an engine for an afternoon. Grilling for the family too… I used to make a whole thing of it, checking the charcoal, telling everybody not to touch the lid.\n\nBut now I just… I can’t make myself care enough to go. People text me about choir or ask if I’m coming by, and I’ll see it light up and just turn the phone over. I don’t even answer half the time. It’s like the part of me that used to want those things just shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean… I don’t like saying it out loud. The thought keeps coming that I should just be dead. Like everybody would be better off if I wasn’t here taking up space.\n\nAnd when I’m alone, that’s when it scares me. Because it’s not just a passing thing sometimes—I start thinking I might actually do something to make it happen. Then I’ll notice some stupid thing, like a crack in the wall or the sound the fridge is making, and I try to focus on that instead, but… yeah. The thoughts are there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how quickly or slowly you're moving compared to before you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… slowed down, definitely. Everything feels like I’m moving through mud, even little things like getting up from a chair or walking to the kitchen.\n\nAnd when someone asks me something, I hear them, I do… but it’s like the words have to travel a long way before I can answer. My wife will be standing there waiting, and I’m just… stuck. It reminds me of when my old truck wouldn’t turn over right away, you know, that pause before it catches—except it’s me. I don’t mean to ignore people. It just takes me a long time to get anything out.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really been keeping track of the number. The scale’s in the bathroom, but it’s one of those old ones that never sits right on the tile, so I don’t trust it much anyway.\n\nMy pants maybe feel a little different some days, but then laundry does that too, you know? My wife used to tell me I should write things down, like mileage on the car or what we spent at the hardware store, but I never did. So… no, I can’t really say I’ve lost a significant amount. I just haven’t noticed for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… all the time. I keep going over it like… like I missed some turn years ago and now everybody’s paying for it.\n\nI think I failed my wife. My kids too. And the church—I used to help with things there, you know, setting up chairs, fixing that old sink in the basement that always leaked no matter what washer you put in it… and now I just feel like I let everybody down.\n\nIt gets bigger than that, though. Like when something bad happens around us, I start thinking it’s because of me. Like it’s punishment because I wasn’t the man I was supposed to be. I know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But it feels true when I’m in it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Restless? No… not like pacing around or anything. It’s more the opposite, I guess.

Everything I do is slowed way down, like my body’s moving through mud. Even just standing up or getting across the room takes… effort. And when somebody asks me something, I hear it, I do, but it’s like my mouth won’t catch up. I sit there trying to answer, and the words take a long time to come out.

It’s strange, because I used to be the kind of guy who couldn’t sit long if there was a loose hinge or a squeaky door to fix, you know… I’d get up and mess with it. Now I just sit there looking at it. So no, not restless. More stuck.

Yeah… I guess that’s right. I don’t really get anything out of things anymore.

I used to look forward to choir practice—funny, because I was never the best singer, just loud enough to help the bass section, you know—and I’d play pickup ball sometimes, or work on cars with a couple guys I know. No… not fidgeting, not really. I don’t feel like I can’t keep still. It’s more like I can’t get moving.

Everything’s slowed down. Like my body’s in mud or wet cement or something. My wife used to tease me for talking too much when we’d be driving—well, not tease exactly, just say I’d turn a five-minute story into twenty—and now I just sit there, trying to answer one thing, and it takes forever.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… I wouldn’t call it an inside restlessness. I don’t feel driven to move around or do things.

It’s more like everything has slowed down. Like I’m trying to move through mud. Even answering you— I hear what you’re asking, but there’s this delay, like the words are stuck somewhere before they come out.

I used to be quicker about things, even just getting up to check something around the house, but now… no, it’s not agitation. It’s more like I’m stuck in place.

I don’t know… I haven’t really paid much attention to sizes. I haven’t gone out buying clothes or anything.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… mostly I’m just worn out. Like, really worn out. Walking from the bedroom to the kitchen feels like I’ve been hauling lumber all day, and I used to know a guy who did that kind of work—he’d come home covered in dust, you know—but I feel like that without doing anything. I rest, I sit down, I lie there, and it just doesn’t give me anything back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have. It’s like I wake up and there’s this heavy, hopeless feeling sitting right in my chest before I even open my eyes. Some days I end up out in the garage just sitting there—there’s this old coffee can full of screws on the shelf, I don’t know why I notice that—and I just start crying. Not even because of one clear thing. It just comes over me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About four months, I think. It kind of started after my wife went back to working full-time, and I was home most weekdays by myself. Funny, I used to think I’d like the quiet—no TV on, nobody asking where the keys are—but it got… too quiet, I guess. So yeah, around four months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t really enjoy much of anything now. I used to look forward to choir, you know, and there was pickup basketball sometimes—nothing serious, just guys running around like our knees were still 25. And I liked working on cars, even just messing with an engine for an afternoon. Grilling for the family too… I used to make a whole thing of it, checking the charcoal, telling everybody not to touch the lid.\n\nBut now I just… I can’t make myself care enough to go. People text me about choir or ask if I’m coming by, and I’ll see it light up and just turn the phone over. I don’t even answer half the time. It’s like the part of me that used to want those things just shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean… I don’t like saying it out loud. The thought keeps coming that I should just be dead. Like everybody would be better off if I wasn’t here taking up space.\n\nAnd when I’m alone, that’s when it scares me. Because it’s not just a passing thing sometimes—I start thinking I might actually do something to make it happen. Then I’ll notice some stupid thing, like a crack in the wall or the sound the fridge is making, and I try to focus on that instead, but… yeah. The thoughts are there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how quickly or slowly you're moving compared to before you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… slowed down, definitely. Everything feels like I’m moving through mud, even little things like getting up from a chair or walking to the kitchen.\n\nAnd when someone asks me something, I hear them, I do… but it’s like the words have to travel a long way before I can answer. My wife will be standing there waiting, and I’m just… stuck. It reminds me of when my old truck wouldn’t turn over right away, you know, that pause before it catches—except it’s me. I don’t mean to ignore people. It just takes me a long time to get anything out.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really been keeping track of the number. The scale’s in the bathroom, but it’s one of those old ones that never sits right on the tile, so I don’t trust it much anyway.\n\nMy pants maybe feel a little different some days, but then laundry does that too, you know? My wife used to tell me I should write things down, like mileage on the car or what we spent at the hardware store, but I never did. So… no, I can’t really say I’ve lost a significant amount. I just haven’t noticed for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… all the time. I keep going over it like… like I missed some turn years ago and now everybody’s paying for it.\n\nI think I failed my wife. My kids too. It’s more the opposite, I guess.\n\nEverything I do is slowed way down, like my body’s moving through mud. Even just standing up or getting across the room takes… effort. I sit there trying to answer, and the words take a long time to come out.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to be the kind of guy who couldn’t sit long if there was a loose hinge or a squeaky door to fix, you know… I’d get up and mess with it. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… mostly I’m just worn out. Like, really worn out. Walking from the bedroom to the kitchen feels like I’ve been hauling lumber all day, and I used to know a guy who did that kind of work—he’d come home covered in dust, you know—but I feel like that without doing anything. I rest, I sit down, I lie there, and it just doesn’t give me anything back.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yeah, I have. It’s like I wake up and there’s this heavy, hopeless feeling sitting right in my chest before I even open my eyes. Some days I end up out in the garage just sitting there—there’s this old coffee can full of screws on the shelf, I don’t know why I notice that—and I just start crying. Not even because of one clear thing. It just comes over me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About four months, I think. It kind of started after my wife went back to working full-time, and I was home most weekdays by myself. Funny, I used to think I’d like the quiet—no TV on, nobody asking where the keys are—but it got… too quiet, I guess. So yeah, around four months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t really enjoy much of anything now. I used to look forward to choir, you know, and there was pickup basketball sometimes—nothing serious, just guys running around like our knees were still 25. And I liked working on cars, even just messing with an engine for an afternoon. Grilling for the family too… I used to make a whole thing of it, checking the charcoal, telling everybody not to touch the lid.\n\nBut now I just… I can’t make myself care enough to go. People text me about choir or ask if I’m coming by, and I’ll see it light up and just turn the phone over. I don’t even answer half the time. It’s like the part of me that used to want those things just shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean… I don’t like saying it out loud. The thought keeps coming that I should just be dead. Like everybody would be better off if I wasn’t here taking up space.\n\nAnd when I’m alone, that’s when it scares me. Because it’s not just a passing thing sometimes—I start thinking I might actually do something to make it happen. Then I’ll notice some stupid thing, like a crack in the wall or the sound the fridge is making, and I try to focus on that instead, but… yeah. The thoughts are there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how quickly or slowly you're moving compared to before you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… slowed down, definitely. Everything feels like I’m moving through mud, even little things like getting up from a chair or walking to the kitchen.\n\nAnd when someone asks me something, I hear them, I do… but it’s like the words have to travel a long way before I can answer. My wife will be standing there waiting, and I’m just… stuck. It reminds me of when my old truck wouldn’t turn over right away, you know, that pause before it catches—except it’s me. I don’t mean to ignore people. It just takes me a long time to get anything out.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really been keeping track of the number. The scale’s in the bathroom, but it’s one of those old ones that never sits right on the tile, so I don’t trust it much anyway.\n\nMy pants maybe feel a little different some days, but then laundry does that too, you know? My wife used to tell me I should write things down, like mileage on the car or what we spent at the hardware store, but I never did. So… no, I can’t really say I’ve lost a significant amount. I just haven’t noticed for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… all the time. I sit there trying to answer, and the words take a long time to come out.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to be the kind of guy who couldn’t sit long if there was a loose hinge or a squeaky door to fix, you know… I’d get up and mess with it. Now I just sit there looking at it. So no, not restless. More stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s right. I don’t really get anything out of things anymore.\n\nI used to look forward to choir practice—funny, because I was never the best singer, just loud enough to help the bass section, you know—and I’d play pickup ball sometimes, or work on cars with a couple guys I know. More stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s right. I don’t really get anything out of things anymore.\n\nI used to look forward to choir practice—funny, because I was never the best singer, just loud enough to help the bass section, you know—and I’d play pickup ball sometimes, or work on cars with a couple guys I know. Even grilling for the family… I used to make a whole thing of it, checking the coals, arguing about sauce.\n\nNow I just… I can’t make myself care enough to go. People text me, and I see it sitting there, and I don’t answer. Not because I’m busy. I just don’t feel that pull to show up anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been fidgeting or having trouble keeping still when you're sitting or lying down?\n\nPatient: No… not fidgeting, not really. I don’t feel like I can’t keep still. It’s more like I can’t get moving.\n\nEverything’s slowed down. Like my body’s in mud or wet cement or something. Even when somebody asks me a simple question, I hear them fine, but then there’s this long space before I can get the words out. My wife used to tease me for talking too much when we’d be driving—well, not tease exactly, just say I’d turn a five-minute story into twenty—and now I just sit there, trying to answer one thing, and it takes forever.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling an internal restlessness or agitation that makes you want to move around or engage in activities?\n\nPatient: No… I wouldn’t call it an inside restlessness. I don’t feel driven to move around or do things.\n\nIt’s more like everything has slowed down. Like I’m trying to move through mud. Even answering you— I hear what you’re asking, but there’s this delay, like the words are stuck somewhere before they come out.\n\nI used to be quicker about things, even just getting up to check something around the house, but now… no, it’s not agitation. It’s more like I’m stuck in place.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your clothes fitting differently or having to buy smaller sizes since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really paid much attention to sizes. I haven’t gone out buying clothes or anything.\n\nMy wife usually notices that kind of thing before I do—like if a shirt’s getting old or if my jeans look… whatever. I’ve got a pair I wear around the house, and they feel how they feel, I guess. I mean, clothes are funny anyway, depending on the brand. I remember buying work pants years ago and one store’s size was completely different from another, so I stopped trusting the numbers.\n\nBut no, I can’t say I’ve noticed needing smaller clothes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 5% of your body weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I haven’t weighed myself, not in any real way.\n\nWe have a scale in the bathroom, but it’s one of those digital ones that flashes numbers and then shuts off too fast, and half the time I don’t trust it anyway because if it’s sitting on the tile wrong it gives a different number. My wife used to keep track of that kind of thing more than me—doctor visits, numbers, all that.\n\nSo… more than five percent, I can’t say. I haven’t noticed it like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. No… not fidgeting, really. I’m not bouncing my leg or feeling like I have to get up.

Walking from the bedroom to the kitchen feels like I’ve been hauling lumber all day, and I used to know a guy who did that kind of work—he’d come home covered in dust, you know—but I feel like that without doing anything. It kind of started after my wife went back to working full-time, and I was home most weekdays by myself. It’s like the part of me that used to want those things just shut off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts about hurting yourself or ending your life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean… I don’t like saying it out loud. The thought keeps coming that I should just be dead. Like everybody would be better off if I wasn’t here taking up space.\n\nAnd when I’m alone, that’s when it scares me. Because it’s not just a passing thing sometimes—I start thinking I might actually do something to make it happen. Then I’ll notice some stupid thing, like a crack in the wall or the sound the fridge is making, and I try to focus on that instead, but… yeah. The thoughts are there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how quickly or slowly you're moving compared to before you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… slowed down, definitely. Everything feels like I’m moving through mud, even little things like getting up from a chair or walking to the kitchen.\n\nAnd when someone asks me something, I hear them, I do… but it’s like the words have to travel a long way before I can answer. My wife will be standing there waiting, and I’m just… stuck. It reminds me of when my old truck wouldn’t turn over right away, you know, that pause before it catches—except it’s me. I don’t mean to ignore people. It just takes me a long time to get anything out.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really been keeping track of the number. The scale’s in the bathroom, but it’s one of those old ones that never sits right on the tile, so I don’t trust it much anyway.\n\nMy pants maybe feel a little different some days, but then laundry does that too, you know? My wife used to tell me I should write things down, like mileage on the car or what we spent at the hardware store, but I never did. So… no, I can’t really say I’ve lost a significant amount. I just haven’t noticed for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… all the time. I keep going over it like… like I missed some turn years ago and now everybody’s paying for it.\n\nI think I failed my wife. My kids too. And the church—I used to help with things there, you know, setting up chairs, fixing that old sink in the basement that always leaked no matter what washer you put in it… and now I just feel like I let everybody down.\n\nIt gets bigger than that, though. Like when something bad happens around us, I start thinking it’s because of me. Like it’s punishment because I wasn’t the man I was supposed to be. I know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But it feels true when I’m in it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having trouble sitting still lately?\n\nPatient: Restless? No… not like pacing around or anything. It’s more the opposite, I guess.\n\nEverything I do is slowed way down, like my body’s moving through mud. Even just standing up or getting across the room takes… effort. And when somebody asks me something, I hear it, I do, but it’s like my mouth won’t catch up. I sit there trying to answer, and the words take a long time to come out.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to be the kind of guy who couldn’t sit long if there was a loose hinge or a squeaky door to fix, you know… I’d get up and mess with it. Now I just sit there looking at it. So no, not restless. More stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s right. I don’t really get anything out of things anymore.\n\nI used to look forward to choir practice—funny, because I was never the best singer, just loud enough to help the bass section, you know—and I’d play pickup ball sometimes, or work on cars with a couple guys I know. Even grilling for the family… I used to make a whole thing of it, checking the coals, arguing about sauce.\n\nNow I just… I can’t make myself care enough to go. People text me, and I see it sitting there, and I don’t answer. Not because I’m busy. I just don’t feel that pull to show up anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been fidgeting or having trouble keeping still when you're sitting or lying down?\n\nPatient: No… not fidgeting, not really. I don’t feel like I can’t keep still. It’s more like I can’t get moving.\n\nEverything’s slowed down. Like my body’s in mud or wet cement or something. Even when somebody asks me a simple question, I hear them fine, but then there’s this long space before I can get the words out. My wife used to tease me for talking too much when we’d be driving—well, not tease exactly, just say I’d turn a five-minute story into twenty—and now I just sit there, trying to answer one thing, and it takes forever.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling an internal restlessness or agitation that makes you want to move around or engage in activities?\n\nPatient: No… I wouldn’t call it an inside restlessness. I don’t feel driven to move around or do things.\n\nIt’s more like everything has slowed down. Like I’m trying to move through mud. Even answering you— I hear what you’re asking, but there’s this delay, like the words are stuck somewhere before they come out.\n\nI used to be quicker about things, even just getting up to check something around the house, but now… no, it’s not agitation. It’s more like I’m stuck in place.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your clothes fitting differently or having to buy smaller sizes since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really paid much attention to sizes. I haven’t gone out buying clothes or anything.\n\nMy wife usually notices that kind of thing before I do—like if a shirt’s getting old or if my jeans look… whatever. I’ve got a pair I wear around the house, and they feel how they feel, I guess. I mean, clothes are funny anyway, depending on the brand. I remember buying work pants years ago and one store’s size was completely different from another, so I stopped trusting the numbers.\n\nBut no, I can’t say I’ve noticed needing smaller clothes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 5% of your body weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I haven’t weighed myself, not in any real way.\n\nWe have a scale in the bathroom, but it’s one of those digital ones that flashes numbers and then shuts off too fast, and half the time I don’t trust it anyway because if it’s sitting on the tile wrong it gives a different number. My wife used to keep track of that kind of thing more than me—doctor visits, numbers, all that.\n\nSo… more than five percent, I can’t say. I haven’t noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling like you need to move around when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: No… not fidgeting, really. I’m not bouncing my leg or feeling like I have to get up.\n\nIt’s more the opposite. Everything I do feels slowed down, like my body’s moving through mud. Even sitting here, when you ask me something, I hear it… I understand the words… but then it takes a while before I can get mine out.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to answer quicker. Even little things, like getting up from a chair, or reaching for something—now there’s this lag. Like my body and my mouth are both behind.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

No, not like pacing or… hand wringing, I don’t think. I’m not wearing a path in the carpet or anything.

My wife used to tell me I should write things down, like mileage on the car or what we spent at the hardware store, but I never did. So… no, I can’t really say I’ve lost a significant amount. I just haven’t noticed for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… all the time. I keep going over it like… like I missed some turn years ago and now everybody’s paying for it.\n\nI think I failed my wife. My kids too. And the church—I used to help with things there, you know, setting up chairs, fixing that old sink in the basement that always leaked no matter what washer you put in it… and now I just feel like I let everybody down.\n\nIt gets bigger than that, though. Like when something bad happens around us, I start thinking it’s because of me. Like it’s punishment because I wasn’t the man I was supposed to be. I know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But it feels true when I’m in it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having trouble sitting still lately?\n\nPatient: Restless? No… not like pacing around or anything. It’s more the opposite, I guess.\n\nEverything I do is slowed way down, like my body’s moving through mud. Even just standing up or getting across the room takes… effort. And when somebody asks me something, I hear it, I do, but it’s like my mouth won’t catch up. I sit there trying to answer, and the words take a long time to come out.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to be the kind of guy who couldn’t sit long if there was a loose hinge or a squeaky door to fix, you know… I’d get up and mess with it. Now I just sit there looking at it. So no, not restless. More stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s right. I don’t really get anything out of things anymore.\n\nI used to look forward to choir practice—funny, because I was never the best singer, just loud enough to help the bass section, you know—and I’d play pickup ball sometimes, or work on cars with a couple guys I know. Even grilling for the family… I used to make a whole thing of it, checking the coals, arguing about sauce.\n\nNow I just… I can’t make myself care enough to go. People text me, and I see it sitting there, and I don’t answer. Not because I’m busy. I just don’t feel that pull to show up anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been fidgeting or having trouble keeping still when you're sitting or lying down?\n\nPatient: No… not fidgeting, not really. I don’t feel like I can’t keep still. It’s more like I can’t get moving.\n\nEverything’s slowed down. Like my body’s in mud or wet cement or something. Even when somebody asks me a simple question, I hear them fine, but then there’s this long space before I can get the words out. I don’t feel driven to move around or do things.\n\nIt’s more like everything has slowed down. Like I’m trying to move through mud. I remember buying work pants years ago and one store’s size was completely different from another, so I stopped trusting the numbers.\n\nBut no, I can’t say I’ve noticed needing smaller clothes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 5% of your body weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I haven’t weighed myself, not in any real way.\n\nWe have a scale in the bathroom, but it’s one of those digital ones that flashes numbers and then shuts off too fast, and half the time I don’t trust it anyway because if it’s sitting on the tile wrong it gives a different number. My wife used to keep track of that kind of thing more than me—doctor visits, numbers, all that.\n\nSo… more than five percent, I can’t say. I haven’t noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling like you need to move around when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: No… not fidgeting, really. I’m not bouncing my leg or feeling like I have to get up.\n\nIt’s more the opposite. Everything I do feels slowed down, like my body’s moving through mud. Even sitting here, when you ask me something, I hear it… I understand the words… but then it takes a while before I can get mine out.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to answer quicker. Even little things, like getting up from a chair, or reaching for something—now there’s this lag. Like my body and my mouth are both behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself unintentionally repeating certain movements, like hand wringing or pacing back and forth?\n\nPatient: No, not like pacing or… hand wringing, I don’t think. I’m not wearing a path in the carpet or anything.\n\nIt’s more that everything feels slowed down. Like I’m trying to move through mud. My wife used to say I’d answer before she finished asking sometimes… now I just sit there.\n\nSo, no repeated movements that I’ve noticed. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, most days, I think. It’s like I know the things I used to enjoy are still there, but they don’t… pull me toward them anymore.

I used to really look forward to choir, and playing pickup ball when the guys got together. I’d work on cars for hours, you know, even if it was just messing with something in the garage. And grilling for the family—that used to be my thing. I’d plan it out, make too much food, everybody hanging around…

Now people text me about showing up, or ask if I’m coming, and I just… I can’t make myself care enough to even answer sometimes. It’s not that I hate those things. It’s just like the feeling part of it is gone.

The thoughts are there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how quickly or slowly you're moving compared to before you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… slowed down, definitely. Everything feels like I’m moving through mud, even little things like getting up from a chair or walking to the kitchen.\n\nAnd when someone asks me something, I hear them, I do… but it’s like the words have to travel a long way before I can answer. My wife will be standing there waiting, and I’m just… stuck. It reminds me of when my old truck wouldn’t turn over right away, you know, that pause before it catches—except it’s me. I don’t mean to ignore people. It just takes me a long time to get anything out.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really been keeping track of the number. The scale’s in the bathroom, but it’s one of those old ones that never sits right on the tile, so I don’t trust it much anyway.\n\nMy pants maybe feel a little different some days, but then laundry does that too, you know? My wife used to tell me I should write things down, like mileage on the car or what we spent at the hardware store, but I never did. So… no, I can’t really say I’ve lost a significant amount. I just haven’t noticed for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… all the time. I keep going over it like… like I missed some turn years ago and now everybody’s paying for it.\n\nI think I failed my wife. My kids too. And the church—I used to help with things there, you know, setting up chairs, fixing that old sink in the basement that always leaked no matter what washer you put in it… and now I just feel like I let everybody down.\n\nIt gets bigger than that, though. Like when something bad happens around us, I start thinking it’s because of me. Like it’s punishment because I wasn’t the man I was supposed to be. I know that probably sounds… I don’t know. It’s more the opposite, I guess.\n\nEverything I do is slowed way down, like my body’s moving through mud. Even just standing up or getting across the room takes… effort. And when somebody asks me something, I hear it, I do, but it’s like my mouth won’t catch up. I sit there trying to answer, and the words take a long time to come out.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to be the kind of guy who couldn’t sit long if there was a loose hinge or a squeaky door to fix, you know… I’d get up and mess with it. Now I just sit there looking at it. So no, not restless. More stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s right. I don’t really get anything out of things anymore.\n\nI used to look forward to choir practice—funny, because I was never the best singer, just loud enough to help the bass section, you know—and I’d play pickup ball sometimes, or work on cars with a couple guys I know. Even grilling for the family… I used to make a whole thing of it, checking the coals, arguing about sauce.\n\nNow I just… I can’t make myself care enough to go. People text me, and I see it sitting there, and I don’t answer. Not because I’m busy. I just don’t feel that pull to show up anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been fidgeting or having trouble keeping still when you're sitting or lying down?\n\nPatient: No… not fidgeting, not really. My wife used to tease me for talking too much when we’d be driving—well, not tease exactly, just say I’d turn a five-minute story into twenty—and now I just sit there, trying to answer one thing, and it takes forever.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling an internal restlessness or agitation that makes you want to move around or engage in activities?\n\nPatient: No… I wouldn’t call it an inside restlessness. I don’t feel driven to move around or do things.\n\nIt’s more like everything has slowed down. Like I’m trying to move through mud. I remember buying work pants years ago and one store’s size was completely different from another, so I stopped trusting the numbers.\n\nBut no, I can’t say I’ve noticed needing smaller clothes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 5% of your body weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. Not really fidgeting, no. I’m not bouncing my leg or needing to get up all the time.

It’s like I wake up and there’s this heavy, hopeless feeling sitting right in my chest before I even open my eyes. Some days I end up out in the garage just sitting there—there’s this old coffee can full of screws on the shelf, I don’t know why I notice that—and I just start crying. Not even because of one clear thing. It just comes over me.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: About four months, I think. It kind of started after my wife went back to working full-time, and I was home most weekdays by myself. Funny, I used to think I’d like the quiet—no TV on, nobody asking where the keys are—but it got… too quiet, I guess. So yeah, around four months.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying things you used to like before you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I don’t really enjoy much of anything now. I used to look forward to choir, you know, and there was pickup basketball sometimes—nothing serious, just guys running around like our knees were still 25. And I liked working on cars, even just messing with an engine for an afternoon. The thought keeps coming that I should just be dead. Like everybody would be better off if I wasn’t here taking up space.\n\nAnd when I’m alone, that’s when it scares me. Because it’s not just a passing thing sometimes—I start thinking I might actually do something to make it happen. Then I’ll notice some stupid thing, like a crack in the wall or the sound the fridge is making, and I try to focus on that instead, but… yeah. The thoughts are there.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how quickly or slowly you're moving compared to before you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… slowed down, definitely. Everything feels like I’m moving through mud, even little things like getting up from a chair or walking to the kitchen.\n\nAnd when someone asks me something, I hear them, I do… but it’s like the words have to travel a long way before I can answer. My wife will be standing there waiting, and I’m just… stuck. It reminds me of when my old truck wouldn’t turn over right away, you know, that pause before it catches—except it’s me. I don’t mean to ignore people. It just takes me a long time to get anything out.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really been keeping track of the number. The scale’s in the bathroom, but it’s one of those old ones that never sits right on the tile, so I don’t trust it much anyway.\n\nMy pants maybe feel a little different some days, but then laundry does that too, you know? My wife used to tell me I should write things down, like mileage on the car or what we spent at the hardware store, but I never did. So… no, I can’t really say I’ve lost a significant amount. I just haven’t noticed for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… all the time. I keep going over it like… like I missed some turn years ago and now everybody’s paying for it.\n\nI think I failed my wife. My kids too. And the church—I used to help with things there, you know, setting up chairs, fixing that old sink in the basement that always leaked no matter what washer you put in it… and now I just feel like I let everybody down.\n\nIt gets bigger than that, though. Like when something bad happens around us, I start thinking it’s because of me. Like it’s punishment because I wasn’t the man I was supposed to be. I know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But it feels true when I’m in it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having trouble sitting still lately?\n\nPatient: Restless? No… not like pacing around or anything. It’s more the opposite, I guess.\n\nEverything I do is slowed way down, like my body’s moving through mud. Even just standing up or getting across the room takes… effort. And when somebody asks me something, I hear it, I do, but it’s like my mouth won’t catch up. I sit there trying to answer, and the words take a long time to come out.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to be the kind of guy who couldn’t sit long if there was a loose hinge or a squeaky door to fix, you know… I’d get up and mess with it. Now I just sit there looking at it. So no, not restless. More stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s right. I don’t really get anything out of things anymore.\n\nI used to look forward to choir practice—funny, because I was never the best singer, just loud enough to help the bass section, you know—and I’d play pickup ball sometimes, or work on cars with a couple guys I know. Even grilling for the family… I used to make a whole thing of it, checking the coals, arguing about sauce.\n\nNow I just… I can’t make myself care enough to go. People text me, and I see it sitting there, and I don’t answer. Not because I’m busy. I just don’t feel that pull to show up anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been fidgeting or having trouble keeping still when you're sitting or lying down?\n\nPatient: No… not fidgeting, not really. I don’t feel like I can’t keep still. It’s more like I can’t get moving.\n\nEverything’s slowed down. Like my body’s in mud or wet cement or something. Even when somebody asks me a simple question, I hear them fine, but then there’s this long space before I can get the words out. My wife used to tease me for talking too much when we’d be driving—well, not tease exactly, just say I’d turn a five-minute story into twenty—and now I just sit there, trying to answer one thing, and it takes forever.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling an internal restlessness or agitation that makes you want to move around or engage in activities?\n\nPatient: No… I wouldn’t call it an inside restlessness. I don’t feel driven to move around or do things.\n\nIt’s more like everything has slowed down. Like I’m trying to move through mud. Even answering you— I hear what you’re asking, but there’s this delay, like the words are stuck somewhere before they come out.\n\nI used to be quicker about things, even just getting up to check something around the house, but now… no, it’s not agitation. It’s more like I’m stuck in place.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your clothes fitting differently or having to buy smaller sizes since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really paid much attention to sizes. I haven’t gone out buying clothes or anything.\n\nMy wife usually notices that kind of thing before I do—like if a shirt’s getting old or if my jeans look… whatever. I’ve got a pair I wear around the house, and they feel how they feel, I guess. I mean, clothes are funny anyway, depending on the brand. I remember buying work pants years ago and one store’s size was completely different from another, so I stopped trusting the numbers.\n\nBut no, I can’t say I’ve noticed needing smaller clothes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 5% of your body weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I haven’t weighed myself, not in any real way.\n\nWe have a scale in the bathroom, but it’s one of those digital ones that flashes numbers and then shuts off too fast, and half the time I don’t trust it anyway because if it’s sitting on the tile wrong it gives a different number. My wife used to keep track of that kind of thing more than me—doctor visits, numbers, all that.\n\nSo… more than five percent, I can’t say. I haven’t noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling like you need to move around when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: No… not fidgeting, really. I’m not bouncing my leg or feeling like I have to get up.\n\nIt’s more the opposite. Everything I do feels slowed down, like my body’s moving through mud. Even sitting here, when you ask me something, I hear it… I understand the words… but then it takes a while before I can get mine out.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to answer quicker. Even little things, like getting up from a chair, or reaching for something—now there’s this lag. Like my body and my mouth are both behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself unintentionally repeating certain movements, like hand wringing or pacing back and forth?\n\nPatient: No, not like pacing or… hand wringing, I don’t think. I’m not wearing a path in the carpet or anything.\n\nIt’s more that everything feels slowed down. Like I’m trying to move through mud. I just haven’t noticed for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty or blame yourself for things that have happened?\n\nPatient: Yeah… all the time. I keep going over it like… like I missed some turn years ago and now everybody’s paying for it.\n\nI think I failed my wife. My kids too. And the church—I used to help with things there, you know, setting up chairs, fixing that old sink in the basement that always leaked no matter what washer you put in it… and now I just feel like I let everybody down.\n\nIt gets bigger than that, though. Like when something bad happens around us, I start thinking it’s because of me. Like it’s punishment because I wasn’t the man I was supposed to be. I know that probably sounds… I don’t know. But it feels true when I’m in it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling restless or having trouble sitting still lately?\n\nPatient: Restless? No… not like pacing around or anything. It’s more the opposite, I guess.\n\nEverything I do is slowed way down, like my body’s moving through mud. Even just standing up or getting across the room takes… effort. And when somebody asks me something, I hear it, I do, but it’s like my mouth won’t catch up. I sit there trying to answer, and the words take a long time to come out.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to be the kind of guy who couldn’t sit long if there was a loose hinge or a squeaky door to fix, you know… I’d get up and mess with it. Now I just sit there looking at it. So no, not restless. More stuck.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure most of the day, nearly every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s right. I don’t really get anything out of things anymore.\n\nI used to look forward to choir practice—funny, because I was never the best singer, just loud enough to help the bass section, you know—and I’d play pickup ball sometimes, or work on cars with a couple guys I know. Even grilling for the family… I used to make a whole thing of it, checking the coals, arguing about sauce.\n\nNow I just… I can’t make myself care enough to go. People text me, and I see it sitting there, and I don’t answer. Not because I’m busy. I just don’t feel that pull to show up anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you been fidgeting or having trouble keeping still when you're sitting or lying down?\n\nPatient: No… not fidgeting, not really. I don’t feel like I can’t keep still. It’s more like I can’t get moving.\n\nEverything’s slowed down. Like my body’s in mud or wet cement or something. Even when somebody asks me a simple question, I hear them fine, but then there’s this long space before I can get the words out. My wife used to tease me for talking too much when we’d be driving—well, not tease exactly, just say I’d turn a five-minute story into twenty—and now I just sit there, trying to answer one thing, and it takes forever.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling an internal restlessness or agitation that makes you want to move around or engage in activities?\n\nPatient: No… I wouldn’t call it an inside restlessness. I don’t feel driven to move around or do things.\n\nIt’s more like everything has slowed down. Like I’m trying to move through mud. Even answering you— I hear what you’re asking, but there’s this delay, like the words are stuck somewhere before they come out.\n\nI used to be quicker about things, even just getting up to check something around the house, but now… no, it’s not agitation. It’s more like I’m stuck in place.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your clothes fitting differently or having to buy smaller sizes since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really paid much attention to sizes. I haven’t gone out buying clothes or anything.\n\nMy wife usually notices that kind of thing before I do—like if a shirt’s getting old or if my jeans look… whatever. I’ve got a pair I wear around the house, and they feel how they feel, I guess. I mean, clothes are funny anyway, depending on the brand. I remember buying work pants years ago and one store’s size was completely different from another, so I stopped trusting the numbers.\n\nBut no, I can’t say I’ve noticed needing smaller clothes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 5% of your body weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I haven’t weighed myself, not in any real way.\n\nWe have a scale in the bathroom, but it’s one of those digital ones that flashes numbers and then shuts off too fast, and half the time I don’t trust it anyway because if it’s sitting on the tile wrong it gives a different number. My wife used to keep track of that kind of thing more than me—doctor visits, numbers, all that.\n\nSo… more than five percent, I can’t say. I haven’t noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling like you need to move around when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: No… not fidgeting, really. I’m not bouncing my leg or feeling like I have to get up.\n\nIt’s more the opposite. Everything I do feels slowed down, like my body’s moving through mud. Even sitting here, when you ask me something, I hear it… I understand the words… but then it takes a while before I can get mine out.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to answer quicker. Even little things, like getting up from a chair, or reaching for something—now there’s this lag. Even now, you ask me something and I hear it, I know what you said, but then there’s this long pause before I can get the words to come out. My wife used to say I’d answer before she finished asking sometimes… now I just sit there.\n\nSo, no repeated movements that I’ve noticed. More like I’m stuck in low gear.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things like pacing back and forth when you're feeling down?\n\nPatient: No… not pacing. Now it’s like there’s a delay in everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure for at least half of the days since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, most days, I think. Yeah… it is. I mean, I’m not working, but even at home I can’t keep up with things.

I used to always have plans, little handyman stuff around the house—fixing a cabinet, patching something, making a list for the garage. I’d buy parts and have it all figured out in my head. Now I just… don’t follow through. Things sit there half-done, or not even started.

I’ve got a pair I wear around the house, and they feel how they feel, I guess. I mean, clothes are funny anyway, depending on the brand. I remember buying work pants years ago and one store’s size was completely different from another, so I stopped trusting the numbers.\n\nBut no, I can’t say I’ve noticed needing smaller clothes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost more than 5% of your body weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I haven’t weighed myself, not in any real way.\n\nWe have a scale in the bathroom, but it’s one of those digital ones that flashes numbers and then shuts off too fast, and half the time I don’t trust it anyway because if it’s sitting on the tile wrong it gives a different number. My wife used to keep track of that kind of thing more than me—doctor visits, numbers, all that.\n\nSo… more than five percent, I can’t say. I haven’t noticed it like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling like you need to move around when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: No… not fidgeting, really. I’m not bouncing my leg or feeling like I have to get up.\n\nIt’s more the opposite. Everything I do feels slowed down, like my body’s moving through mud. Even sitting here, when you ask me something, I hear it… I understand the words… but then it takes a while before I can get mine out.\n\nIt’s strange, because I used to answer quicker. Even little things, like getting up from a chair, or reaching for something—now there’s this lag. Like my body and my mouth are both behind.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself unintentionally repeating certain movements, like hand wringing or pacing back and forth?\n\nPatient: No, not like pacing or… hand wringing, I don’t think. I’m not wearing a path in the carpet or anything.\n\nIt’s more that everything feels slowed down. Like I’m trying to move through mud. Even now, you ask me something and I hear it, I know what you said, but then there’s this long pause before I can get the words to come out. My wife used to say I’d answer before she finished asking sometimes… now I just sit there.\n\nSo, no repeated movements that I’ve noticed. More like I’m stuck in low gear.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself doing things like pacing back and forth when you're feeling down?\n\nPatient: No… not pacing. I don’t really go back and forth like that.\n\nIt’s more like I slow down. Everything feels heavy, like my body’s moving through mud or something. Even when somebody asks me a question, I hear it, I know they asked me, but then I’m just sitting there trying to make the words come out.\n\nI used to be quicker than this. Now it’s like there’s a delay in everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure for at least half of the days since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, most days, I think. It’s like I know the things I used to enjoy are still there, but they don’t… pull me toward them anymore.\n\nI used to really look forward to choir, and playing pickup ball when the guys got together. I’d work on cars for hours, you know, even if it was just messing with something in the garage. And grilling for the family—that used to be my thing. I’d plan it out, make too much food, everybody hanging around…\n\nNow people text me about showing up, or ask if I’m coming, and I just… I can’t make myself care enough to even answer sometimes. It’s not that I hate those things. It’s just like the feeling part of it is gone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been fidgeting a lot or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Not really fidgeting, no. I’m not bouncing my leg or needing to get up all the time.\n\nIt’s kind of the opposite, I guess. Everything I do has slowed way down, like my body’s moving through mud. Even answering you— I hear the question, I understand it, but then there’s this gap before I can get the words out.\n\nIt’s strange because I used to be quick with things, you know… I’d be halfway into an answer before someone finished talking. Now I just sit there, like the words are stuck somewhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your depression is interfering with your ability to work or take care of things at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it is.
